- Title, `romaji` (small muted line beneath the title, omitted cleanly when absent - issue #47), JLPT chip, a `MasteryRing` reading the point's own `GrammarProgress.entry.memoryStrength` (`0` if not yet introduced), `shortExplanation`/`longExplanation`, `formation` (safe to show here, unlike on the grammar quiz cards - this is a study page, not a recall test), and every one of the point's example sentences (`jp`/`romaji`/`en`)
- **Chapter locator** (issue #58): a small "Chapter N · x of y" line beneath the romaji line, linking to `/grammar/chapters?chapter=<id>` (the chapter browser, pre-expanded to that chapter). Resolved via `GrammarService.loadTeachingOrder()` directly rather than `loadChapterFor` - see Services & Business Logic → Grammar Activity: Chapter progress display for why. Silently absent when the order can't be loaded or the point isn't in it (e.g. excluded/untestable).
- `GrammarDifferentiator` (issue #58) now also renders here, directly above `GrammarRelatedPointsCard` - see Services & Business Logic → Grammar Activity: Chapter progress display for why the two stay separate components shown together rather than merged.
- **Interactive example sentences**: `jp` renders via the shared `InteractiveSentence` component (the same one a word page's `WordSentence` uses for vocab sentences) instead of plain text, so every resolved word (`GrammarExampleWord.vocabId !== null`) is clickable through to `/vocab/:id`, exactly like on `VocabDetailScreen`. `GrammarExample`'s shape (`words[]`, no `matches` offsets) differs from `Sentence`'s, so `grammarSentence.ts in @gokan/dataset-schema`'s `grammarExampleToSentence(example, id)` adapts one into the other (the grammar quiz cards use the same adapter) - deriving each match's `start`/`length` from cumulative `surface` length rather than hand-tracking offsets, relying on the words-concatenate-to-`jp` invariant the dataset guarantees. A conjugated token keeps its stored reading only when it is the surface's own (`surfaceReading`): the data stores the lemma's on some (食べ carries たべる), which shown as furigana read the token wrong. No `targetVocabId` is passed (unlike vocab's usage, which highlights the vocab the page itself is about) since a grammar example has no single "target" vocab word - every resolved word gets the same clickable-dashed-underline treatment. The point's own grammar-pattern markers ARE highlighted, though (issue #89): `grammarSentence.utils.ts`'s `patternHighlightRanges(example)` derives the marker's character offsets from `GrammarExample.patternWordIndices` (the same cumulative-`surface`-length technique, merging consecutive pattern words into one contiguous range) and passes them as `InteractiveSentence`'s `highlightRanges` prop - safe here, unlike on the grammar cloze's blanks, since this is a study page with no answer to leak.
- **Register cue (issue #42 follow-up)**: when set, `formalityLevel` renders as a small chip next to the JLPT chip (a label lookup, e.g. `'very-formal-literary'` → "Very formal / literary") and the full `usageNote` renders as a paragraph beneath the title - unlike the grammar cloze, which shows the note only once answered, there's no answer to leak here, so both render unconditionally when present
- **"More examples from the corpus" (issue #73 follow-up)**: a collapsible section below the curated example sentences surfacing the same corpus-mined pool `computeBlankPlan`'s productivity selection draws review sentences from (see Services & Business Logic → Grammar Dataset's "Corpus-mined sentences" note) - without this, the mined data could only be inspected by grinding a point to its 2nd review. Loaded via `GrammarService.loadMinedExamples(point.id)` in its own `useEffect` keyed on `grammarId`; renders nothing when the point has no pool (the common case - ~350/828 points). Collapsed by default (a pool can hold up to 60 sentences), then capped to 5 with a "Show all N" toggle once opened. Renders each mined `GrammarExample` through the exact same `grammarExampleToSentence`/`patternHighlightRanges` → `InteractiveSentence` path the curated examples use (including the same pattern highlighting) - full sentence, no blanks, since this is a read-only browsing view, not the quiz's recall test.
- An "Add to Grammar Queue" button when the point hasn't been introduced yet, dispatching the same `GRAMMAR_INTRO_CHOICE`-backed `grammarActions.saveGrammarIntroChoice(point, 'learn')` action `GrammarIntroCard`'s "Learn" button uses - mirrors `VocabDetailScreen`'s "Add to Learning List"
- Once introduced, a Stats card (reviews, interval, introduced date, next review) plus the shared `SRSHistoryGraph` (see below) plotted against the point's single `entry`
- **Shared related-entry base (`components/RelatedEntriesCard.tsx`)**: `GrammarRelatedPointsCard` and `GrammarVariantsCard` render through this one presentational component, and a word page's Explore panels (Related, Synonyms) through its rows alone (`RelatedEntryList`), so the vocab and grammar detail pages' related-entry lists share the exact same Card chrome, row layout (a Japanese `primary`, an inline `secondary` node, an optional right-aligned `meta`, an optional clamped `description`), hover treatment, header link, and cap/expand control. Each caller resolves its own data (and owns the lazy-load + expand state, since only the displayed rows are fetched) and maps it to `RelatedEntry` rows; nothing visual is duplicated per page.
- `GrammarRelatedPointsCard` (`pages/grammar/GrammarRelatedPointsCard.tsx`, issue #42 follow-up, adapted to the named `family` schema in issue #45): the point's near-synonym **family** (`family.relatedPoints`, same core idea at a different formality/nuance) - fetches each related id via `GrammarService.loadGrammarPoint`, renders `family.name` as the header (generic "Related Points" fallback only when `family` is absent) and a clickable list (kana-only title + JLPT chip) to `/grammar/:grammarId` through the shared base, cap/expand at `INITIAL_COUNT = 5`, plus the contrast-gated "Compare when to use each" link. Returns `null` when `family?.relatedPoints` is empty/absent. A realization variant now has no `family`, so this card is empty on a variant page and the point's forms are surfaced by `GrammarVariantsCard` instead.
- `GrammarVariantsCard` (`pages/grammar/GrammarVariantsCard.tsx`): the point's **realization variants** - the SAME point written differently (それでは / それじゃ / じゃ), a distinct relationship from the near-synonym family. Reads `variant-groups.json` via `GrammarService.loadVariantGroups()` keyed on `point.variantOf ?? point.id`, then lists every OTHER group member (fetched for its `jlptLevel`) with a relation chip ("Base form" for the canonical, "Contraction"/"Politeness"/"Particle"/... for the rest) + JLPT chip, under the header "Other forms", through the same shared base. From a variant it links back to its canonical and sibling realizations; from the canonical it links to every realization. Returns `null` when the point has no variant group. This is what made じゃ/それじゃ/それでは reachable from each other on the detail page - previously `GrammarDetailScreen` had no variant awareness at all, so a variant page was a disconnected orphan
- Linked from `GrammarIntroCard`'s title and, once a card is answered, from the "View grammar point" link on the grammar cards (`PointLink`; the study card shows it at once) - deliberately **not** the quiz card's title, since RC4 removed that specifically to stop leaking the answer (see Application Pages → Grammar Screen)

**`SRSHistoryGraph`** (`components/SRSHistoryGraph.tsx`, renamed from `VocabHistoryGraph.tsx`): generalized to plot an arbitrary list of `{key, label, entry, color}` series against one shared time axis, rather than being hardcoded to vocab's reading/meaning pair - a `GrammarProgress.entry` is shaped exactly like a `VocabProgress.reading`/`meaning` entry for this purpose, so `GrammarDetailScreen` reuses the same component with a single-entry series instead of a second implementation. `VocabDetailScreen` passes reading and meaning, plus production once that entry has been activated; `GrammarDetailScreen` passes one (grammar). The vocab page's Next Review list and mastery rings follow the same rule: production shows a date once activated and the literal text **"Not started"** before that, rather than being hidden. An absent row reads as the feature being broken, which is exactly how the first release was reported. "Activated" means `isProductionActivated`, **not** a pending `dueDate`: a mastered production entry has no due date, and gating on one hid the ring and graph series of every word whose production was finished or skipped (issue #94). A mastered entry reads "Mastered" in the Next Review list. The production ring uses `MasteryRing`'s own `variant="production"` (`THEME.mastery.production.loop1`), the Vocabulary list shows it once activated, `ReviewTimeline` ("Review History") lists production reviews alongside reading and meaning, and the Vocabulary list sorts by "Production mastery" and counts production mistakes in its Most mistakes sort (`countWrongReviews`, `utils/winRate.utils.ts`).

### Listening Library (`pages/listening/`)

Routes `/listening` and `/listening/:mediaId`. Tells the learner which anime fit what they know now and tracks their progress through them: a list of anime (four picked by hand plus Jiten's 100 easiest eligible ones, see Build & Development → Media Dataset) with, for each one and each episode, how much of its vocabulary the learner already knows. Not a study session: nothing here is scheduled or graded, and it is not session-route-aware (leaving `/quiz` for it ends a session like any other non-consult page).

- **What "known" means** (`buildWordKnowledge`, `utils/mediaCoverage.utils.ts`): a word in `learningQueue` with `introductionAt` set, split into `mastered` (`isVocabFullyMastered`, settings-aware) and `learning`. A queued-but-never-introduced word is unknown.
- **Two figures, both over Gokan vocabulary only** (`computeCoverage`): `occurrences` (weighted by how often each word is used: how much of what is said uses known words, the headline percentage) and `unique` (each distinct word once). Both come as `{ mastered, learning, total }`, the exact shape `ChapterProgressBar` takes, so the bars reuse it rather than a new component. A series figure is the episodes' words summed (`aggregateEpisodeWords`).
- **The vocabulary-only caveat is shown on both pages** (`VocabularyOnlyNote`, `listeningShared.tsx`): particles, kana-only words and loanwords are not Gokan vocabulary yet (a product decision for the prototype), so roughly a third of an episode's distinct words are left out and the figure must not read as overall comprehension. It also reads differently from Jiten's own coverage figure, which counts every word.
- **`ListeningLibraryScreen`**: every title (`MediaService.loadIndex` + `loadLibraryWords`), searched then sorted by series coverage, best fit first, by **`rankLibrary`** (then the titles being watched moved to the top, see below), the one ranking function the Main hub's covers also use, so the two cannot disagree. Ranking is per learner (it depends on the words they know), so it cannot be precomputed in the dataset; `library.json` carries the word lists it needs (~0.5 MB compressed at 104 titles). A smaller "top 200 words per title" index was measured and rejected for now: its order agreed only approximately (top 4 usually 3 of 4 the same) and its percentages read high, while saving ~400 KB; worth revisiting at several hundred titles. The search box, the genre row (placeholder pills at the widths the genres measure) and the count line render from the first frame, with skeleton rows where the titles go, so the list does not jump down when the library arrives (#134). A skeleton row is the loaded row's frame (`LIST_FRAME`, `ROW_FRAME`, shared by both) with a `MediaCoverPlaceholder` and bars where its text goes, so the list keeps its shape as well as its place. Each card shows the headline percentage, a three-way bar, distinct words known, genres, and its status with the next episode to watch (the first unwatched one, since episodes are sequential). **Filters** (`filterLibrary`): "I like" genre chips (`libraryGenres`, most common first) saved to `UserSettings.listeningGenres` via `saveSettings`, any-of matching, "Show all" clears them; and a search box (not persisted).
- **Search** (`filterLibrary`, `titleSearchKey`): the query and every title form (original, its kana `reading`, AniList's romaji, English) and the tags go through one key, then the query must be a substring, so a title is found however it is typed: NFKC and lowercase (a full-width ３), kana in romaji with the shared `kanaToRomaji` (hiragana, katakana and romaji meet in one alphabet; kanji stay as written), long vowels folded (`ou` and doubled vowels to one: `jouzu` is `jozu`, AniList's own `Kyoryuu` and `Kyouryuu` meet), then only letters, digits and kanji kept (`takagisan`, `shin chan`). The folding runs before the spaces go, so a long vowel never forms across two words (いもうと うまる would otherwise fold to `imotomaru` and `umaru` would miss it). The app never reads kanji itself (kuromoji's dictionary is 17 MB): the dataset's `reading` is what lets `たかぎ`, `タカギ` and `takagi` find 高木さん. A kuromoji misreading (古見 as ふるみ) needs no fix, since kana is compared as romaji and AniList's romaji holds the right reading (`こみさん` finds 古見さんは、コミュ症です。).
- **Order: titles being watched first** (`watchingByTitle`, `watchStatus`, `watchingFirst`): a title is **Watching** from its first episode marked watched until every episode is, then **Completed**. The library page puts the Watching titles first, the most recently marked first (the latest `updatedAt` among the title's watched marks, which `mergeWatchedEpisodes` merges per episode, so every device agrees), then every other title, Completed ones included, in `rankLibrary`'s order. Unmarking every episode puts a title back where its coverage ranks it. A title being watched shows **whatever the genre filter**: the page runs `rankLibrary` with the search only, and `watchingFirst` keeps every Watching title (in any genre) and filters the rest by genre (`matchesGenres`). The filter is saved, so one set weeks ago would otherwise hide the show the learner is in the middle of. The search still applies to a Watching title, and a Completed one is not pinned and follows the filter. The count line counts only the titles in the chosen genres as matching them and names the rest ("N of M titles matching your genres, plus K you are watching"). `rankLibrary` itself stays the best-fit ranking, so the hub's "Best match" never names the show being watched.
- **Each row's status**: under the title, `StatusDot` in the learning-state steps, the 35% accent dot for **Watching · N of M episodes · Episode K next**, the solid dot for **Completed**, nothing for a title not started. The episode count is in the status line, not under the bar, where it read as the bar's caption (the bar is words known); each part keeps its words together, so at 320px the line wraps after a separator, with the dot beside its first line. On a phone the figures (percent known, words to learn) sit under the title instead of in a right-hand column, so the title keeps the width beside the cover; from `sm` they stand on the right. `check:layout` marks one title watched in full and one in part and measures the library with both rows.
- **`ListeningTitleScreen`**: a summary (romaji/English title, year, episode count, speech speed with its band, genres, series coverage, links to AniList, MyAnimeList and the Jiten deck, and a **Mark all watched** / **Unmark all** button that sets every episode in one `setEpisodesWatched` call, each with its own `coverageAtWatch`), a collapsible **words to learn for the whole series** panel, then one row per episode with its coverage, speech band, a Watch/Watched toggle, and its own expandable words-to-learn panel.
- **Words to learn** (`WordsToLearn`): the unknown words (`unknownWords`) of the episode or the whole series, sorted by `sortWords` (`utils/wordOrder.utils.ts`), 10 at first with "Show 20 more". Each row links to `/vocab/:id`, shows the times used, a **new kanji** mark when the learner's queue could not introduce it yet (`isLearnableNow`), and a Learn button calling the same `saveVocabIntroChoice(vocab, 'learn')` as "Add to Learning List"; a learned word becomes known and drops off. One **Order** select, shared by every panel on the page and kept in `sessionStorage` (`gokan.listening.wordSort`): **Your learning order** (default), Most used here, General frequency (JPDB rank), JLPT level (N5 first, no level last), Kanji order (KKLC step).
- **"Your learning order"** mirrors the vocab queue: words it could introduce now come first (all kanji known, or the KKLC step reached under the kklc order, or always when `ignoreKnownKanjiRequirement` is on), ordered by the learner's `preferredLearningOrder`: frequency rank, JLPT then rank, KKLC step then rank, or for kanji_coverage the queue refill's own score (`kanjiCoverageScoresFor`, over the same `kanjiCoverageScores`; see Learning Queue Logic). It is the score of the refill's first pick rather than its greedy loop, close enough for a list a learner picks from. The per-word facts come from the frequency, JLPT and KKLC indexes, loaded by `useWordOrdering` (`pages/listening/`) through `VocabularyService`'s own caches; until they load, every sort falls back to most-used-here.
- **Speech speed bands** (`speechSpeedLabel`, morae per minute): under 260 Slow, under 320 Moderate, under 380 Brisk, else Fast. Set from the spread in Jiten's data (learner channels ~180-220, calm anime ~230-300, fast native talk 400+).
- **Covers** (`MediaCover`, `listeningShared.tsx`): each title's AniList cover, loaded from AniList's CDN at display time (`referrerPolicy="no-referrer"`, lazy), on the library cards, the title page and the hub card. All three of AniList's widths go in a `srcset` (`coverSrcSet`, `utils/coverImage.utils.ts`) and `MediaCover`'s `size` prop names the placement (hub, library, title page), whose entry in one table holds both the width classes and the matching `sizes`, so the two cannot drift apart and the browser fetches the smallest file that fills the box: a 40px hub thumbnail takes the 100px file (about a quarter of the 230px one's bytes). AniList's CDN serves JPEG/PNG whatever the `Accept` header, so a modern format would need an image proxy; the art is not ours to re-encode and host. The box keeps a 2:3 poster shape filled with AniList's dominant `color`, which also stays as a plain block if the image fails or a title has no cover, so nothing reflows. The artwork is the studios': never bundled or copied into the dataset, only linked and credited.
- **Design**: deliberately not Jiten's card layout (a large cover beside a stats grid and two bars): ours is a small thumbnail with one headline figure and one bar. No synopses are shown (Jiten's come from Crunchyroll and are not part of its CC BY-SA data). **Attribution** is rendered by `JitenCredit` on both pages: Jiten for the vocabulary (CC BY-SA 4.0), AniList for the covers, artwork © its studio.

### Statistics Screen (`pages/stats/StatsScreen.tsx`)

- `StatsOverview` - headline counters (including Kanji Coverage). "Global Win Rate" covers every quiz type (reading, meaning, production, grammar); it counted reading and meaning only
- `QuizTypeWinRates` - "Win Rate by Quiz": each quiz type's win rate over all logged reviews, its recent calibration window, and its growth level (see Services & Business Logic -> SRS calibration)
- **Layout**: `max-w-6xl`, and from `lg` up the section cards pair into two columns, each row pairing charts of the same shape so neither card is half empty (review forecast / daily progression, the two bar charts, first; then win rates / knowledge curve; then vocab / grammar JLPT coverage); the 151-row chapter coverage spans both, and the two lists stay full width below. One column on a phone.
- `KnowledgeCurveChart` - cumulative knowledge held over time (steady growth vs. plateau); see `utils/knowledge.utils.ts`
- `JlptCoverageChart` - five stacked bars (N5 at top → N1), each showing mastered / in-progress / untouched against that level's total, off `index/jlpt.json`. "Mastered" uses `isVocabFullyMastered` so the split matches the scheduler's definition rather than reimplementing it. Rendering (headline, legend, bars, `<details>` table) lives in a shared `JlptCoverageBars` component (`pages/stats/components/JlptCoverageBars.tsx`, issue #32 follow-up, generalized further in issue #58) taking `rows: JlptLevelRow[]` (`{ key: string | number, label: string, mastered, learning, total }` - `key` is the row's stable identity, `label` the short text next to its bar, e.g. `"N5"` or a chapter's `"Ch. 14"`) plus an `itemLabel` string and optional `headline`/`keyColumnLabel` overrides - `JlptCoverageChart` only computes `rows` from vocab-shaped data (`learningQueue` + `isVocabFullyMastered`), passing `key`/`label` as the level number and `"N{level}"`. One hue in two steps (solid accent + 35% accent) rather than two hues, since the segments are ordinal stages and the design system reserves the secondary accent for errors; a legend and direct `n / total` labels carry the distinction so it never rests on color alone. Past `maxVisibleRows` (default 10) the bar list scrolls in a fixed-height pane instead of growing the page - irrelevant at 5 JLPT levels, load-bearing at 148 chapters (see below)
- `GrammarJlptCoverageChart` (issue #32 follow-up) - grammar's equivalent, same `JlptCoverageBars` renderer, computing `rows` from `grammarQueue` + `grammar/index/jlpt.json` (`GrammarService.loadJlptIndex()`) + `isGrammarFullyMastered` instead. Kept as a separate component rather than folded into `JlptCoverageChart` since the index shape (no `containedKanji`) and mastery predicate (no `settings` param) genuinely differ between the two activities - only the bar/legend/table rendering is shared
- `GrammarChapterCoverageChart` (`pages/stats/components/GrammarChapterCoverageChart.tsx`, issue #58) - a chapter-axis coverage view, same `JlptCoverageBars` renderer again, one row per chapter (`key: chapter.id, label: "Ch. {n}"`) via `computeGrammarChapterProgress` off `GrammarService.loadTeachingOrder()`'s chapters. See Services & Business Logic → Grammar Activity: Chapter progress display for why chapters are now a more meaningful coverage axis than JLPT level. Links out to the chapter browser via a "Browse the full curriculum" button
- `DailyProgressionChart` - per-day review activity (correct/incorrect), last 14 days, built on the shared `buildDailyActivity` helper (`utils/activity.utils.ts`) also used by the Main hub's `DailyActivityCard`. Since issue #32's follow-up, `buildDailyActivity` folds in `entry.history` off `grammarQueue` alongside `reading.history`/`meaning.history` off `learningQueue`, so both this chart and the daily activity card reflect grammar reviews too, not just vocab ones - there is no separate grammar-only variant
- `ReviewForecast` - upcoming review load (Now / later today / next 6 days), one stacked segment per direction: reading, meaning and production, with a legend since the three are steps of one hue. Bucketing lives in the pure `buildReviewForecast` (`utils/reviewForecast.utils.ts`), which decides relevance the way `scheduling.ts` does: meaning skipped when meaning quizzes are off, production when production quizzes are off or the entry was never activated, and any mastered entry skipped whatever its stored due date (issue #94; production used to be missing from the chart entirely)
- The Vocabulary and Grammar lists are not on this page: see the Vocabulary list (`/vocab`) and the Grammar home's All points tab (`/grammar/browse`).

---

## gokan-dictionary App

`apps/gokan-dictionary` is a separate, standalone Svelte + Vite app (own `package.json`, own tests) implementing [issue #19](https://github.com/gokan-dev/gokan-srs/issues/19) and overhauled by [issue #118](https://github.com/gokan-dev/gokan-srs/issues/118): static, crawlable pages for every word, kanji and grammar point in the compiled dataset, for visitors who want to look something up without going through gokan-srs's setup flow, often arriving from a search engine. It shares code with gokan-srs only through packages (`@gokan/dataset-schema`: the dataset types, headwords, romaji, sentence segmentation) and the dataset submodule both consume; no gokan-srs UI, routing or quiz logic is reachable from it. Its visual rules are docs/DESIGN_SYSTEM.md section 8.

### Deployment and the base path

The dictionary is served as a **subfolder of the gokan-srs site**, at `gokan-srs.com/dictionary/`, not as its own subdomain. With no established domain authority yet, consolidating every link and ranking signal onto one hostname is worth more than the operational tidiness of a separate host, and the dictionary's whole purpose is to accumulate authority that also lifts the app.

- `src/lib/site.ts` owns both `SITE_ORIGIN` and `BASE_PATH` (default `/dictionary`). Setting `VITE_BASE_PATH=''` builds for a bare origin instead, which is the only source change a move to a subdomain would need. Both are read via `import.meta.env`, **not** `process.env`: Vite statically replaces `VITE_`-prefixed reads in the client bundle (`src/client/search.ts` reaches `site.ts` through `urls.ts`), while Bun resolves the same expression at prerender time, so one declaration serves both contexts without a `typeof process` guard.
- `BASE_PATH` affects **generated URLs only, never the on-disk layout** under `dist/`. `prerender.ts` still writes `dist/vocab/{id}/index.html`; the prefix is supplied by the deploy step, which syncs `dist/` into the `dictionary/` key prefix of the app's S3 bucket. Everything that builds an href, canonical, or sitemap entry goes through `src/lib/urls.ts` so the two can never disagree. The Vite build uses `base: './'`, so the URLs inside the built CSS (the web font) and between scripts are relative and hold under any base path.
- **No `robots.txt` is generated in subfolder mode** (`sitemap.ts`'s `shouldEmitRobotsTxt()`). Crawlers only ever fetch `robots.txt` from a host root, so a file at `/dictionary/robots.txt` would never be read by anything. `apps/gokan-srs/public/robots.txt` is the authoritative file for the whole host and declares both sitemaps.
- Both apps deploy from **one shared workflow** (`.github/workflows/deploy-site.yml`) - see Build & Development below for why, and for the `--delete` hazard that shared bucket creates.

### Static generation model

There is no client-side router or SSR framework (SvelteKit, Next.js, etc.) - the resolved decision on issue #19 was "build-time pre-rendering... kept deliberately lightweight". Every page is a plain static `index.html` file, generated once at build time by `scripts/prerender.ts` and served by any static host with no server runtime. Every page is complete without JavaScript; only search and the furigana toggle need it.

- `scripts/svelte-ssr-loader.ts` registers a Bun runtime plugin that compiles `.svelte` files with `svelte/compiler`'s `generate: 'server'` mode directly - independent of the Vite build entirely, since Svelte's own `svelte/server` `render()` only turns a component into an HTML *fragment*. It must be imported for its side effect *before* any `.svelte` import, so `prerender.ts` imports the pages dynamically (`await import(...)`).
- `scripts/prerender.ts` (the generator) resolves the compiled dataset (via `dataset.server.ts`, see Dataset consumption), renders each page component with `svelte/server`'s `render()`, wraps the fragment in a document via `documentShell.ts` (SEO meta, canonical link, JSON-LD, asset links; `noindex` for `/search/`), and writes `dist/vocab/{id}/`, `dist/kanji/{character}/`, `dist/grammar/{id}/`, `dist/grammar/chapters/{id}/`, `dist/grammar/family/{id}/`, the browse pages (`dist/vocab/`, `dist/vocab/jlpt-n{5..1}/` and their numbered pages `dist/vocab/jlpt-n1/2/`..., `dist/kanji/`, the kanji learning order pages `dist/kanji/orders/`, `dist/kanji/orders/{order}/` and `dist/kanji/orders/{order}/{first}-{last}/`, `dist/grammar/`, `dist/grammar/families/`, `dist/grammar/chapters/`, `dist/grammar/conjugations/`), `dist/search/`, `dist/index.html`, `dist/sitemap.xml` (every page but `/search/`) and the search index (`dist/data/search-core.json`, `dist/data/search-full.json`). About 39,250 files in under 20 seconds.
  - **Svelte's hydration markers are stripped** (`stripHydrationMarkers`): `<!--[-->`, `<!--[0-->` and `<!--]-->` bracket every block for hydration, which never happens here; on a list of 600 words they were a tenth of the page.
  - **Japanese is checked**: `japaneseWithoutLang` runs over every page body and prerender fails on Japanese-only text whose nearest `lang` is not `ja`, or an element with the `jp` class lacking `lang="ja"` (design system section 8 says why). Pages mark Japanese with `lang="ja"` directly; the CSS sets every `[lang="ja"]` element in the Japanese face. Dataset text that may be Japanese or English (a chapter's title, a lesson's) gets its `lang` from `langOf`.
  - **Kanji directory names are the raw UTF-8 character, never percent-encoded** - static hosts decode a request URL's percent-escapes before resolving a file on disk, so writing `dist/kanji/%E6%80%9D/` instead of `dist/kanji/思/` would break navigation from a real percent-encoded href. `urls.ts`'s `kanjiPath()` percent-encodes for embedding in href/canonical/sitemap strings; the filesystem write path uses the raw character.
  - Cross-page links (a word's "Made of"/"Used in" lists, a kanji's words, the JLPT lists) are resolved through a one-pass `Map<id, VocabSummary>` built by `vocabSummary.ts` before the main per-page loop (the same pass builds each word's search row), keeping memory bounded to small summaries instead of ~36k parsed vocab files. Ids that do not resolve are dropped with one aggregate warning: a best-effort batch generator, not an interactive app serving one user's data.
- `vite.config.ts` builds only the browser-facing assets the pages link, each an explicit input so Vite content-hashes and manifests it (`dist/.vite/manifest.json`, read by `prerender.ts`): the stylesheets (`src/styles/app.scss` plus one per page type) and two plain TypeScript scripts (`src/client/search.ts` on every page, `src/client/grammarFilter.ts` on `/grammar/`). There is no Svelte plugin in it: a `.svelte` import reaching a client script fails the build rather than shipping a Svelte runtime, and `scripts/check-build.ts` checks no client asset contains one. `bun run dev` builds and previews; there is no dev-server shell.
- `bun run build` ends with `scripts/check-build.ts`: every size budget (`lib/budgets.ts`, design system section 8), one web font, no Svelte runtime, and the search acceptance queries of issue #118 run against the real built index ("eat" finds 食べる in the top 3, "taberu" and "食べた" put it first, "生" lists 生, 生活, 先生 and 学生 in its first 10 words and the kanji 生, "から" finds the grammar point から), and those of issue #139 (romaji typed partway: "tabe", "taber", "benky", "nihong" list the word in their top 3, "chot" ちょっと in its top 5; "car" and "house" put 車 and 家 before any romaji prefix match). Any miss fails the build.
- Page components (`src/pages/*.svelte`) have **no `<style>` blocks**: component CSS extraction cannot work for components compiled outside Vite's module graph. Styling lives in `src/styles/`, as SCSS, split one stylesheet per page type (see Styling conventions).

### Styling conventions

**SCSS, split per page, no duplication.** These are project guidelines, not incidental structure.

```
src/styles/
├── _tokens.scss              # variables + mixins ONLY, emits no CSS
├── app.scss                  # :root theme tokens, the web font + every rule shared by 2+ page types
└── pages/
    ├── home.scss             kanji.scss        grammar.scss            grammar-family.scss   # family page + families index
    ├── vocab.scss            kanji-index.scss  grammar-index.scss      grammar-conjugations.scss
                              kanji-orders.scss grammar-chapters.scss   # chapter page + curriculum index
```

Every page links `app.scss` plus, if it has one, its own page stylesheet (`documentShell.ts`'s `pageStylesheetHref`). The vocabulary index, the JLPT lists, the kanji orders hub and `/search/` are built entirely from shared rules and link nothing extra.

**Why per page, beyond tidiness:** the stylesheet's content hash is embedded in every page's `<link>`, so a rule added to `app.scss` changes the bytes of all ~39k pages and re-uploads the entire site (see Deployment). A rule added to `pages/vocab.scss` re-uploads only the vocab pages. `app.scss` also has a 10 KB budget, the tightest of all.

**Where a rule belongs:** in `app.scss` only if two or more page types genuinely use it. If two pages look similar because they copied each other, fix the duplication rather than promoting the copy.

**Sass deprecations fail the build.** `vite.config.ts` passes every deprecation Sass currently warns about (status `active`) as `fatalDeprecations`, so slash division, `@import` and the like are errors, not a warning that scrolls past in CI (#134).

**`_tokens.scss` emits no CSS.** It holds only Sass variables and mixins. Two kinds of token, deliberately split:
- **Sass variables** (`$space-2`, `$bp-narrow`, `$target`) for values needed at *build* time; media query conditions are the load-bearing case, since a custom property cannot be used in one.
- **CSS custom properties** (`--accent`, `--surface`, `--font-mincho`) for values that change at *runtime* (the dark theme, under `prefers-color-scheme`) or that would otherwise repeat in every rule (the font stacks). They are declared once, in `app.scss`'s `:root`, with the same colour values as gokan-srs's `index.css`. The stacks go through `meta.inspect`: interpolated plainly, Sass drops the quotes around "Source Serif 4", which makes the whole stack invalid.

**TypeScript everywhere, always typed.** No `.js` files beyond `svelte.config.js`, and no implicit `any`. `bun run --cwd apps/gokan-dictionary typecheck` (svelte-check) must report 0 errors and 0 warnings; it covers `.svelte` files as well as `.ts`.

### Search

**The index is the dictionary's own**, built at prerender by `lib/searchIndex.ts` from the compiled data rather than copied from `index/search.json` (which the app keeps using): ranking needs each word's frequency rank, JLPT level, common flag and conjugation class, which that file does not carry. Format (`lib/types.ts`): words are compact arrays `[id, headword, secondary form, gloss, jlpt, flags]` in frequency order (`index/frequency.json`), so a word's position is its rank; the gloss is the first sense's glosses joined with "; " and cut at a word boundary to 60 characters; flags hold common, learned-in-kana and the conjugation class (`lib/searchRow.ts` reads them). Kanji are `[character, jlpt, frequency]`, grammar points `[id, title, romaji, short explanation, jlpt]` in teaching order. In the files, words are stored **column by column** (`SearchWordColumns`): 288 KB gzipped for the core against 379 KB as one array per word.

Two files, so most lookups never download the long tail: `search-core.json` holds every JLPT word, the 6,500 most frequent words (`CORE_FREQUENT_WORDS`), every kanji and every grammar point; `search-full.json` the remaining ~25,000 words. Read after the core, the full file's rows rank after it, which is their place anyway.

**Loading** (`client/search.ts`): nothing is fetched before the search field is first focused. Then the core index and the query engine (`lib/searchQuery.ts` with the romaji table and the conjugation rules, a separate chunk) load together; after the first query has run, the full index loads in the background and the current query re-runs when it arrives.

**Matching** (`lib/searchQuery.ts`, pure, the one module behind the header panel, `/search/` and the build check): the query is normalised (NFKC, trimmed, Latin lowercased, katakana and hiragana compared as equal for readings) and read every way a learner might have meant it: as written, as romaji converted to kana (`readRomajiQuery`, see the app's search; only when the whole query converts), as a conjugated form (`deinflect` from `lib/conjugation.ts`, kept only for words of the class the rule needs, e.g. 食った for the godan 食う but not 食いた), and as English against the glosses. Tiers, best first: exact headword or reading; the romaji's kana exactly; a deinflected dictionary form (the row says "from 食べた"); a headword or reading prefix (and, for a kanji query, a compound containing it, so "生" finds 先生 and 学生 among its first results); an English sense equal to the query without a leading "to" or article; the query as a whole word of the gloss (`containsWholeWord`, shared with the app); a reading starting with the romaji's kana, its last syllable possibly unfinished (`romajiReadingPrefixes`, see the app's search ranking: "taber" finds たべる; after the English tiers: "house" reads as ほうせ); then any substring. Within a tier: JLPT words, then common words, then frequency. Kanji results are the query's kanji; grammar results match the title without its ～ placeholders, the romaji, or the explanation.

**Conjugation** (`lib/conjugation.ts`): one table of paradigms (ichidan; godan by ending; する, 来る in kanji and kana, 行く, ある, 問う/請う, い-adjectives and いい on よ) used both ways: `conjugate` builds the labelled forms of a word page's Forms table, `deinflect` reads the table backwards, two steps deep through a derived form's own class (食べられなかった: past negative of the ichidan 食べられる, the potential of 食べる). Dictionary-local: gokan-srs's `inflection.utils.ts` generates unlabelled forms for grading and belongs to its exercise engine.

**UI**: the field is a plain `<form role="search">` to `/search/`, so Enter opens the results page even without the script. The script makes it a complete combobox: a grouped panel (Words up to 8, Kanji up to 3, Grammar up to 3, then "See all results for ..."), options with ids and `aria-selected`, `aria-activedescendant` on the input, arrow keys, Enter on a highlighted row opening it, Escape closing, a live region announcing the count. No results says so, with the kana a romaji query was read as and links to the JLPT lists. On phones the panel takes the screen below the field. `/search/` (`noindex`, not in the sitemap) renders every match for `?q=`, 50 words a page, and without JavaScript explains that search needs it and links the browse pages. The home page's hero is the same search field, and the header leaves its own out there.

### Entry pages

- **Word page** (`VocabPage.svelte`): the header answers meaning, reading and level (headword alone in the `<h1>`, the reading, a deduplicated summary gloss from `lib/senses.ts`, neutral chips). **Meanings** are numbered, part of speech in plain English (`shortTagLabel`) above a sense only when it changes, usage labels after the glosses, the first five visible, the rest in "All N meanings", and senses tagged `RARE_SENSE_TAGS` last in "Rare and archaic meanings (N)". **Forms** (verbs and い-adjectives whose headword fits their class) is the `conjugate` table with each form's kana under it. **Example sentences** are ordered at prerender (`lib/sentenceOrder.ts`: translated first, then the headword's own spelling, then shortest, under six characters last), five shown and up to ten more in "More examples (N)", with furigana over every resolved word with kanji. The aside lists the kanji used and the "Made of"/"Used in" words (10 visible, the rest in a disclosure). Two columns from 56rem, split by importance.
- **Sentences**: a vocab sentence's `matches` and a grammar example's `words[]` (through `grammarExampleToSentence`) both go through the shared `segmentInteractiveSentence`, then `rubyParts` (`@gokan/dataset-schema`, the same split the app's `Ruby` component uses) splits each word's reading so the ruby sits over its kanji only (食(た)べる; 引(ひ)き出(だ)し), falling back to the whole reading over the whole word when the kana do not line up (marked `fallback`, rendered `data-ruby-fallback`, which check:layout exempts from its kanji-only rule). `rt` carries `padding-inline: 0.1em` so neighbouring readings never touch, and `ruby` is `white-space: nowrap` so a wrapping word never splits its reading from its kanji (design system section 3). Every resolved word links to its entry: example sentences are the largest source of internal links, what makes the ~36k word pages reachable by a crawler. The furigana toggle is in `client/search.ts` (design system section 8).
- **Kanji page** (`KanjiPage.svelte`): the character alone in the `<h1>`, the JLPT chip, a meta line ("JLPT N5 · KKLC #99 · Frequency #5,839"), and every word containing it, most frequent first: 30 with glosses, the rest in "All N words" as headword and reading only (一 lists 660 words; with glosses they would not fit the page's 80 KB budget).
- **Grammar point page** (`GrammarPage.svelte`): formation as a highlighted panel, the short explanation as the lead, "When to use" (the usage note), the long explanation when it differs, example sentences (furigana, the pattern highlighted through `patternHighlightRanges`, translation, romaji), the conjugation table of an inflection point (`grammar/conjugations.json`), "Other forms" (its realization variants, `lib/grammarVariants.ts`), its family, and corpus examples (`grammar/mined/{id}.json`, capped at 25) in a disclosure. The chapter locator ("Chapter N: title, x of y", `lib/grammarChapters.ts`) sits in the header.
- **Family page** (`GrammarFamilyPage.svelte`, `lib/grammarFamilies.ts`): one per `family.id` (123), not gated on having contrast lessons: every member with its short explanation and level, so even a family without lessons compares its members, then the interchangeable-forms note and the authored lessons.

### Browse pages and internal linking

Every page type has a short path from the site root; orphan pages (sitemap-listed, linked from nowhere) are indexed worse, and this is how a reader browses a dictionary.

- **Home**: the search hero, four browse tiles (two by two), the most frequent N5 words, the counts, a short About.
- **Vocabulary**: `dist/vocab/` links one list per JLPT level (five tiles in one row on desktop). Each list is frequency-ordered and split into pages of 500 words (`lib/pagination.ts`, `vocabJlptPath(level, page)`), every page in the sitemap: N1 was 666 KB on one page. Only ~7,300 of the ~36,000 words carry a level; the others are reachable through search, kanji pages and example sentences.
- **Kanji**: all 2,300 characters as a grid grouped by JLPT level, with jump links; each tile's accessible name is "学, JLPT N5" (the level visually hidden, the character marked Japanese). The levels after N5 are `content-visibility: auto` (`styles/pages/kanji-index.scss`, #134): 3,128 tiles cost 1.4 s of style and layout on a slowed phone CPU. Each level's placeholder height comes from its tile count (`--tiles`) and the grid's columns at that width, counted so it is never short (a short one clips tiles, which axe reads as obscured touch targets); measured 3 to 25% taller than the level from 320 to 1280px. The tiles stay in the DOM, so find-in-page, screen readers and the jump links work as before.
- **Kanji learning orders** (issue #147): one page set per order in `KANJI_ORDERS`, answering "which words can I read now that I am at step N of my kanji book". `/kanji/orders/` lists every order (label, description, kanji and word counts), linked from the home page and the kanji index; `/kanji/orders/{order}/` (the id in kebab case, `kanjiOrderSlug`) cuts the order into ranges of 100 steps (`lib/kanjiOrders.ts`, `kanjiRanges`), each with its first kanji and counts; `/kanji/orders/{order}/1-100/` shows the range's kanji as tiles, then each step's kanji with the words it unlocks, a step pager (`Pager.svelte` with range labels) and a link into the app. Titles and descriptions come from `seo.ts` (`kanjiOrdersMeta`, `kanjiOrderMeta`, `kanjiRangeMeta`), naming an order by its `searchName` in `ORDER_INFO` ("KKLC", "JLPT kanji order"). Which words a step unlocks is `kanjiStepIndex` from `@gokan/dataset-schema`, the function the app's kanji-driven order uses for every method but KKLC (which reads the dataset's `index/kklc.json`, built by the same rule; the two differed at one step of 2,300 when this was written). A range late in an order unlocks over 3,000 words, so each step lists its `WORDS_PER_KANJI` (5) most frequent, then "N more at this step" linking to the kanji's page: one page per range keeps the URLs simple, and the cap is what holds a range page within its `kanjiOrderPage` budget (the heaviest, `jlpt/301-400/`, 115 KB of 150). A kanji page links each book position ("#123") to the range page holding it (`rangeOfStep`); Essential Kanji, a book but not an order, stays unlinked.
- **Grammar**, four pages sharing one set of tabs (`GrammarTabs.svelte`: By level, Families, Curriculum, Conjugations): `/grammar/` lists every point grouped by level in teaching order as compact rows (`GrammarList.svelte`, `lib/grammarBrowse.ts`), with a filter field and level toggles in plain TypeScript over the static rows (`client/grammarFilter.ts`, about 1 KB, no data download; hidden without the script); `/grammar/families/` lists the families with their members; `/grammar/chapters/` is the curriculum, chapters under level headings, each chapter with its own page and a previous/next pager; `/grammar/conjugations/` is one table per word type (verbs, い-adjectives, な-adjectives): form, pattern (linked to its point), one example and level (`lib/grammarConjugations.ts`).
- The interactive Svelte grammar browser this replaced crashed in production (its client bundle included Svelte's server runtime) and cost 41 KB of runtime plus 340 KB of JSON duplicating the page; the static pages and a 1 KB filter do the same job.

### Dataset consumption

Reads the same `gokan-dataset` submodule as gokan-srs, resolved independently: `src/lib/dataset.server.ts`'s `resolveCompiledDir()` walks up to the shared `apps/gokan-srs/dataset/compiled` path and auto-runs `git submodule update --init` if it's missing, since gokan-dictionary's own CI does not check out submodules - this is the one remaining place that can fetch it before a build needs real data. Node-only and never imported from a `.svelte` component. Dataset types come from `@gokan/dataset-schema`, the package gokan-srs uses, so the two apps cannot disagree on the dataset's shape. Everything above is built from compiled data that already exists: kanji meanings, readings and stroke counts, audio and pitch accent are not in it.

### Tests

Vitest, same as gokan-srs. Every module in `src/lib` has a colocated test (enforced); the scripts (`prerender.ts`, `check-build.ts`, `svelte-ssr-loader.ts`) and the client scripts are thin glue, verified by running the build (which runs the search acceptance queries against the real index) and by a browser pass over every page type at 320, 375 and 1440px, light and dark, with and without JavaScript.

- `conjugation.test.ts`: for every word class and irregular, `deinflect` of every form `conjugate` builds contains the dictionary form; two-step readings; no bare-ending false matches.
- `searchQuery.test.ts`: each tier and its order, katakana/hiragana equality, romaji and its "Searched as" kana, class-checked deinflection, English senses over gloss mentions, JLPT-first within a tier, kanji and grammar results. `searchIndex.test.ts`, `searchRow.test.ts`: the row format, the columnar layout round trip and the core/full split.
- `senses.test.ts`, `sentenceOrder.test.ts`, `furigana.test.ts` (sentence runs; the ruby split and its fallback are tested with `rubyParts` in the package), `vocabSummary.test.ts`, `grammarBrowse.test.ts`, `grammarConjugations.test.ts`, `grammarChapters.test.ts`, `grammarFamilies.test.ts`, `grammarVariants.test.ts`, `pagination.test.ts`, `budgets.test.ts`.
- `documentShell.test.ts` (escaping, noindex, marker stripping, the `lang="ja"` check), `urls.test.ts` (every path carries the base prefix exactly once; `BASE_PATH` interpolated rather than hardcoded so a subdomain build does not fail them falsely; `absoluteUrl` resolves against the bare origin), `seo.test.ts`, `sitemap.test.ts`, `dataset.server.test.ts` (loaders against fixtures under `src/lib/__fixtures__/compiled/`).

## Build & Development

### Commands

Run from the **monorepo root** (`bun install` there installs deps for every workspace app). The root `package.json` proxies the common `gokan-srs` commands directly; anything else runs via `--cwd apps/gokan-srs` (or `--cwd apps/gokan-dictionary`).

**Development:**
```bash
bun run dev                              # Start gokan-srs dev server (Vite)
bun run typecheck                        # gokan-srs TypeScript type checking
bun run lint                             # gokan-srs ESLint
bun run dictionary:dev                   # Build gokan-dictionary and preview it
```

**Build:**
```bash
bun run build                            # gokan-srs production build
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-srs preview     # Preview gokan-srs production build
bun run dictionary:build                 # gokan-dictionary production build
```

**Testing:**
```bash
bun run test                             # Run all gokan-srs tests (Vitest)
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-srs test:watch  # Run gokan-srs tests in watch mode
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-dictionary test # Run gokan-dictionary tests
```

### Deployment

Both apps ship to one environment from **one reusable workflow**, `.github/workflows/deploy-site.yml`, called by `deploy.yml` (production, gated on both test suites) and `deploy-staging.yml` (staging, on any `*-rc*` tag or a manual dispatch of any ref, deliberately ungated). gokan-srs lands at the bucket root; gokan-dictionary lands under the `dictionary/` key prefix, matching the `/dictionary/*` CloudFront behavior in `apps/gokan-srs/terraform/`.

**One workflow, not one per app**: both apps read the same compiled dataset from the same submodule checkout, so a single checkout guarantees they ship from one dataset version. Two workflows could deploy the app from one commit and the dictionary from another, leaving an environment serving two apps built off different datasets. **One workflow, not one per environment**: staging and production would otherwise hold two copies of this logic and drift the same way, one level up. Callers pass only what differs (bucket, distribution, origin).

**Neither app uses `aws s3 sync`.** Both are deployed by `packages/deploy/scripts/deploy-s3.ts` (`--site srs` or `--site dictionary`), which hashes every built file, compares against a manifest stored beside the site (`.build-manifest.json` at the bucket root, `dictionary/.build-manifest.json`), and uploads only what differs. The pure logic (diff, cache-control, upload order) is `packages/deploy/src/deployManifest.ts`, unit-tested.

The reason is that `aws s3 sync`'s default comparison uploads a file whose local mtime is newer than the S3 object's, and CI rebuilds from a fresh checkout every run: every one of the dictionary's ~38k generated pages looked new on every deploy, so even a deploy touching only the SRS app re-uploaded the entire dictionary, roughly 20 minutes each time. A dataset change touching 200 entries now uploads 200 files.

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **`--size-only` is not a safe substitute, and this is the trap worth remembering.** It skips every file whose byte length did not change, and both apps are full of edits that keep the length. Asset filenames are content-hashed to a fixed length, so a CSS-only change produces page HTML of *identical byte length*, and every page would keep pointing at a pruned stylesheet. A dataset edit often swaps one character for another: gokan-srs's data files *were* synced with `--size-only`, and a vocab entry whose `"jlptLevel":1` became `5` stayed N1 in production. Comparing content hashes catches both by construction; `deployManifest.test.ts` carries both as regression cases.

**Each site prunes only keys its own previous manifest listed.** gokan-srs deploys to the bucket root and the dictionary lives under `dictionary/` in the same bucket; the old `aws s3 sync --delete` needed a load-bearing `--exclude "dictionary/*"` to avoid wiping all dictionary pages. The manifest makes that impossible by construction, since the app's manifest never lists a dictionary key.

The approach depends on the builds being reproducible: two consecutive full dictionary rebuilds produce byte-for-byte identical output across all ~39,250 files, so a rebuild with no source or dataset change yields zero uploads. If a future change introduces a timestamp, a build id, or any other nondeterminism into the output, this silently degrades back to re-uploading everything.

Operational details worth knowing:
- The manifest is uploaded **last**, only after every upload and delete has succeeded. An interrupted deploy therefore leaves the previous manifest in place and the next run redoes the work, rather than believing files it never uploaded are present.
- A missing or unreadable manifest (the first deploy of a site) falls back to the bucket listing: S3 reports a single-part upload's ETag as the MD5 of its content, which is the hash the manifest uses, so only files that really differ are uploaded. The listing never drives deletions (a root listing contains the dictionary). If the listing fails too, everything is uploaded.
- HTML is uploaded **last** within a deploy: a page names the hashed assets (and, in gokan-srs, the dataset version) it needs, so it must not go live before them.
- **Sitemaps get their `<lastmod>` at deploy time.** The stored manifest keeps, per key, its upload hash, its page content hash (`pageContentHash`: a page's HTML with every quoted reference to a hashed file under `assets/` blanked out; any other file's plain hash) and the UTC day that content hash last changed (`changeDates`: carried forward while it is unchanged, today when it differs or was never dated, so the first deploy after this dates everything that day). Dating by bytes would re-date all ~40k dictionary pages on any stylesheet or script edit, since each links the hashed files; a change to shared header or footer text still re-dates every page, which is accurate. After hashing and before diffing, the built `sitemap.xml` (both sites; built with no dates, and a dated one is refused) is rewritten with each URL's date (`datedSitemap`): a dictionary URL is its `index.html`, a gokan-srs route with no file of its own is the SPA shell, so `/` and `/about` are dated by the shell's own text (its meta and noscript text, the injected analytics tag), not by what the bundle renders: with its asset references blanked, an edit to the About copy, which lives in the JS bundle, leaves their date unchanged. Google trusts `lastmod` only while it is accurate, so it is never the build date, which would also break the reproducible build. A deploy with no content change writes the same sitemap, which is then not re-uploaded. A manifest from before dates were kept (a bare map of hashes), or from before content hashes were kept (no `content`), still reads, with nothing to date by.
- Changed files are hardlinked into a staging tree (`packages/deploy/.deploy-stage/`) so one recursive CLI upload moves exactly the intended set; per-file `aws` invocations would take hours on a full run.
- Cache-control per file class: HTML always revalidates; content-hashed assets (`assets/`) are immutable for a year; files that keep their name across releases get a 1-hour TTL (gokan-srs's `data/compiled/` and root files, the dictionary's `data/search.json` and `sitemap.xml`). The dictionary's remaining files keep the year-long immutable header.

**gokan-srs versions its dataset requests.** Every dataset URL is built by `datasetUrl()` in `src/services/http.ts` (enforced by the conventions check), which appends `?v=` and the dataset submodule's pinned commit, injected by `vite.config.ts`. Dataset files keep their names across releases, so without it a browser answers a request from a cached older file; the files were served immutable for a year, so that could last a year. A new dataset is a new URL. The 1-hour TTL covers the one gap the version cannot: CloudFront's `CachingOptimized` policy leaves the query string out of the edge cache key, so a request for the new version that reaches the edge before the invalidation lands gets the old file, and the browser keeps it under the new URL only until the hour runs out.
- The manifest lives under `dictionary/` so gokan-srs's root `--delete` pass cannot prune it.
- `--dry-run` reports what would be uploaded and deleted without touching S3.

**gokan-srs knows its own version** (issue #171). `apps/gokan-srs/release/changelog.ts` lists every shipped change, newest first (version, day, one sentence, the issue); its newest entry is the app's version. A change that ships adds one entry at the top: the next minor, the one its rc tags carry. `vite.config.ts` refuses a list out of order (`changelogProblems`), injects `__APP_VERSION__` (read only as `APP_VERSION` in `services/release.service.ts`) and writes two unhashed files to the site root: `version.json` (`{ version, dataset }`) and `changelog.json`; the dev server serves both too. They are root files, so they get the 1-hour TTL and stay out of the bundle.

- **What's new** (`/whats-new`, `pages/about/WhatsNewScreen.tsx`) reads `changelog.json` through `fetchJson` and narrows it once (`parseChangelog`). The footer and About show the version and link to it; like About it is shown before setup.
- **The update banner** (`components/UpdateBanner.tsx`, `hooks/useUpdateAvailable.ts`): `watchLiveVersion` asks for `version.json` (`no-store`) on mount, on each route change, when the tab comes back into view and every 10 minutes, at most once a minute. When the live version differs from the running one, a neutral, dismissible notice offers Reload; never an automatic reload (an answer being typed would be lost), and never while a study session is in progress, paused on a consult page included (`hasSessionInProgress` in `quizSelectors.ts`): a session's task set lives only in the quiz state, so a reload ends it. The reload is safe because `index.html` always revalidates while assets are immutable. Since the CDN keeps `version.json` up to its hour and the invalidation does not list it, a tab may learn of a deploy up to an hour late.
- **The chunk-load guard** (`services/chunkLoadGuard.ts`, installed in `main.tsx`): Vite fires `vite:preloadError` when a lazy page's chunk fails, which is what a tab left open across a deploy hits once the deploy pruned its chunks. The guard reloads once, and not again within a minute (the time is kept in `sessionStorage`, which survives the reload), so a page that still fails (offline, a broken deploy) does not loop. It can still reload a consult page while a session is paused there (the detail pages are lazy), ending that session; that is accepted, since the page is broken without the reload. The banner is what keeps a chosen reload from ending one.
- Why a poll and not a WebSocket, and why the version is committed rather than tagged: docs/MODIFICATION_LOG.md, 2026-10-28.

Environment-specific details worth knowing:

- `VITE_SITE_ORIGIN` is passed per environment, so staging emits staging canonicals rather than claiming to be production.
- `VITE_UMAMI_WEBSITE_ID` is passed by production only (the `UMAMI_WEBSITE_ID` repository variable). Both apps put the Umami Cloud script in their pages through `analyticsScriptTag` (`@gokan/analytics`): gokan-srs from a `transformIndexHtml` plugin in `vite.config.ts`, the dictionary from `renderDocument`. Without the id the tag is empty, so local, CI and staging builds never count a visit. The tag carries `data-domains="gokan-srs.com"`, so even a production build served from another host (a `vite preview`, a mirror of the dictionary) counts nothing. Umami is cookieless and counts only browsers that run JavaScript, which keeps crawlers out of the numbers; the earlier idea of counting distinct IPs in CloudFront logs counted crawlers, merged everyone behind one IP and missed every in-app navigation. Beyond page views, gokan-srs sends one event, `session_started` (`services/analytics.ts`, with `activity: vocab | grammar`), so the dashboard tells learners from visitors. The app's module script runs before the deferred Umami tag, so an event sent before `window.umami` exists (a reload or the PWA opening straight on `/quiz`) is queued and sent when the script's `load` fires; a page without the tag queues nothing, and a blocked script drops the queue.
- `NODE_ENV: production` is set **inside** `deploy-site.yml`, because a called workflow does not inherit the caller's `env` and `apps/gokan-srs/vite.config.ts` keys off it to skip the `vite-plugin-checker` pass.
- Staging's CloudFront distribution attaches an `X-Robots-Tag: noindex, nofollow` response headers policy to the whole site. `staging.gokan-srs.com` serves the same built `robots.txt` as production, so `robots.txt` alone cannot keep crawlers out, and a second complete crawlable copy of ~39k pages would compete with production for the same content.
- Two unhashed dictionary files (`data/search.json`, `sitemap.xml`) are re-uploaded with a 1-hour TTL after the immutable pass, so a dataset change actually reaches search and crawlers.

**CloudFront directory-index rewrite**: the `/dictionary/*` behavior carries a CloudFront Function that appends `index.html` to directory-style URLs. This is load-bearing, not cosmetic. The dictionary emits one `<path>/index.html` per entry, and the OAC origin reaches S3 through the **REST** endpoint, which - unlike the S3 *website* endpoint - performs no directory-index resolution. Without the rewrite, `/dictionary/vocab/1589350/` looks up a key that does not exist, 404s, and is swallowed by the SPA `custom_error_response`, so every dictionary page would quietly serve the SRS app. `default_root_object` does not cover this; it only applies to `/`.

**Known limitation**: `custom_error_response` is distribution-wide in CloudFront, so a genuinely missing `/dictionary/*` URL still returns the SPA shell with a 200 (a soft 404). Every dictionary URL is generated and sitemap-listed, so this should only be reachable through a stale link after a dataset change. Fixing it properly means moving the SPA fallback off `custom_error_response` and into a function on the default behavior, a riskier change to live app routing than the problem currently warrants.

**Dataset** (delegates into the `gokan-dataset` submodule - see below):
```bash
bun run dataset:sync                      # Copy dataset/compiled/ -> public/data/compiled/ (also runs automatically before dev/build)
bun run dataset:build                     # Regenerate the dataset from raw sources, then sync (~1-2 min)
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-srs build:kanji  # Compile KKLC kanji only (delegates into the submodule)
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-srs build:jlpt   # Rebuild only index/jlpt.json (delegates into the submodule)
bun run --cwd apps/gokan-srs build:grammar # Rebuild compiled/grammar/ from the vendored hanabira snapshot (delegates into the submodule; see Grammar Dataset below)
```

### Agent pipeline

Issues become merge-ready PRs through agent workflows in `.github/workflows/`, each started by a GitHub event. A **round** is one pushed commit, reviewed and triaged. Every agent job queues on its branch's lock (AGENTS.md), so they never overlap on a branch, and a review always sees the finished push.

1. **Implement** (`claude-implement-issue.yml`): the `ready-for-dev` label starts it. It opens the PR on `agent/issue-<n>`.
2. **PR review** (`claude-review.yml`), on every push. The reviewer **reports and never pushes**: an obvious fix pushed by a reviewer would start a new round while a decision on the same PR is still open, so every point goes through triage. Its `round` job then ends the review half of the round:
   - If the round's changes ship something (since the branch's last rc, or since `main` for the first round), it tags the next rc. The staging deploy follows, then the **staging review** (`claude-review-staging.yml`), which also only reports, then triage.
   - If they ship nothing (CI, docs, tests, check scripts), the deployed pages are the ones already reviewed: no rc, no staging deploy, no rendered review, straight to triage.

   Which paths ship is one rule, `shipsInDeployment` in `packages/deploy/src/deploymentPaths.ts`, not a judgement in each prompt. The agents no longer tag; a person may still tag an rc by hand, and its staging review then hands the round to triage.
3. **Triage** (`claude-triage.yml`): it reads the round's reviews, the issue, the diff and every earlier triage and decision on the PR. Its agent has no shell and no network: it writes an outcome and a brief, and the workflow's own steps act on them.
   - `done`: nothing to change. The PR gets `agent-done`, and the maintainer is mentioned.
   - `apply`: every point has one obvious resolution. The brief is posted as an `@claude` request, which starts step 4.
   - `decide`: a point needs the maintainer's choice. The brief lists options as checkboxes (one recommended), the PR gets `needs-decision`, and the maintainer is mentioned. The obvious fixes wait with it, so nothing moves until the answer. Ticking the brief's Send box, or replying on the PR (with any note), sends the answer: the `relay` job posts it as an `@claude` request and removes the label.
4. **Address feedback** (`claude-address-feedback.yml`): applies the brief, and only the brief, and pushes, which starts a new round at step 2. A decision to stop, or one that leaves nothing to change, pushes nothing, so no round follows.

**Rounds end.** A review can always find one more polish point, so three things stop the loop: the triage never raises again a point an earlier triage left out ("Not in this change") or the maintainer decided; from round 2 on only real defects and points about the last round's changes count, so a round of polish is a `done` round; and after `MAX_AUTO_ROUNDS` (2) `apply` rounds without a word from the maintainer, the next `apply` becomes a `decide` that offers to stop. Any human comment, decision or `decide` brief restarts that count.

**Only deterministic steps write `@claude`.** That string starts the feedback agent, so no agent prompt may write it; the triage and relay steps post it with `AGENT_GITHUB_TOKEN` (a member's comment, which starts workflows), and post the maintainer's notifications with `GITHUB_TOKEN` (which starts none, and so cannot loop). The rc tag is pushed with `AGENT_GITHUB_TOKEN` too, since a `GITHUB_TOKEN` tag push deploys nothing. The maintainer is the `AGENT_HUMAN` repository variable, or the PR's author when it is unset. A PR labelled `no-triage`, or still a draft, is not triaged; **Agent: triage reviews** can also be run by hand on a branch from the Actions tab.

**Every agent run reports its token usage** in its job summary (`scripts/agent-usage.ts`, read from the execution file claude-code-action writes): the input, cache and output split, and the tool results that filled the context most. Numbers and paths only, since the logs are public. The agents get AGENTS.md from CLAUDE.md's import (the `project` settings source, kept by the conventions check), so no prompt asks them to read it again.

### Dataset Consumption

The compiled dataset (kanji/vocab/sentences/indexes) is **not owned by this repo**. It lives in the separate [`gokan-dataset`](https://github.com/gokan-dev/gokan-dataset) repo - raw sources, the build pipeline (Kuromoji tokenization, JMDict/KKLC/JPDB/JLPT processing), and the compiled output all live there, documented for third-party consumption independent of this app in that repo's `docs/SCHEMA.md`.

`gokan-srs` consumes it as a **git submodule** at `apps/gokan-srs/dataset/` (a public repo, so CI needs no extra credentials to check it out - `submodules: true` on `actions/checkout` is sufficient):

- `apps/gokan-srs/scripts/sync-dataset.ts` copies `dataset/compiled/` → `public/data/compiled/` via a plain `fs.cpSync` - no transformation, since both sides agree on the shape. This runs automatically before `dev` and `build` (chained in `package.json`'s scripts), so `public/data/compiled/` is **no longer committed** to this repo (`.gitignore`'d) - it's purely a synced build artifact, regenerated on demand.
- `apps/gokan-srs/package.json`'s `build:data`/`build:kanji`/`build:jlpt`/`build:jpdb` scripts delegate into the submodule (`bun --cwd dataset build:data`, no `run` keyword - `bun --cwd <dir> run <script>` doesn't reliably execute the script, it's `--cwd`'s own flag scoped to `run`, not a global one composable this way) so the dataset can still be rebuilt from raw sources without leaving the monorepo, then re-sync automatically (`build:data` chains the sync at the end).
- The submodule is **outside** the root workspace glob (`apps/*` only matches direct children of `apps/`), so the root `bun install` does not install its dependencies. Run `bun install --cwd apps/gokan-srs/dataset` once before `build:data`/`build:kanji`/`build:jlpt`/`build:jpdb` will work (not needed for plain `dev`/`build`, which only read the already-committed `compiled/` output via the sync step, never execute anything inside the submodule).
- After cloning fresh, run `git submodule update --init --recursive` (or clone with `--recurse-submodules`) before `bun install`/`bun run dev` - otherwise `sync-dataset.ts` fails fast with a clear error rather than silently serving stale/missing data.
- Vitest's config (`vite.config.ts`) explicitly excludes `dataset/**` from its test glob, since the submodule has its own independent test suite and CI (would otherwise get picked up and double-run as part of `bun run test` here).
- Bumping which `gokan-dataset` commit this repo points to is a normal two-step submodule workflow: commit + push inside `apps/gokan-srs/dataset/` first (a separate repo), then commit the resulting pointer change here.

### Grammar Dataset

The grammar dataset (issue #17) follows the same split as vocab/kanji/sentences: raw source, build pipeline, and compiled output all live in the **`gokan-dataset`** submodule, synced into `public/data/compiled/grammar/` by the normal `sync-dataset.ts` step - there is no `gokan-srs`-side data or build script for it.

- **Source**: [hanabira.org-japanese-content](https://github.com/tristcoil/hanabira.org-japanese-content) (Creative Commons, attribution required - see the credit link on the About page), vendored as a frozen snapshot at `dataset/data/raw/grammar/grammar_ja_{N5,N4,N3,N2,N1}_full_alphabetical_0001.json` (828 grammar points total: N5 136, N4 124, N3 132, N2 191, N1 245). "Vendored snapshot vs. periodic re-sync" was the one ingestion detail issue #17 left open at implementation time; a vendored snapshot was chosen (re-running `build:grammar` against a manually-refreshed `raw/` snapshot is the update path if hanabira's content ever needs a refresh), matching every other raw source in the dataset repo.
- **`dataset/scripts/build-grammar.ts`**: reads the raw JSON, assigns stable ids (`${level}-${index}`, e.g. `"n5-001"` - the upstream dataset has no ids of its own, and since the snapshot is frozen these stay stable across rebuilds), and tokenizes every example sentence to resolve each content word (`名詞`/`動詞`/`形容詞`/`副詞` POS tags only; particles/symbols always stay literal) against the already-compiled `compiled/index/search.json`, capturing each word's `baseForm` (kuromoji's dictionary form) alongside `surface`/`reading`. **Word matching reuses the submodule's own `SentenceTokenizer`** (the same compound/deinflection-aware matcher `build-data.ts` uses for vocab sentences), rather than a lighter grammar-only pass - an earlier design choice (see the removed `[2026-02-28]`-era rationale, now reversed for consistency: every sentence in the app, vocab or grammar, is tokenized identically, so clicking a word behaves and matches the same way in both places). This resolves 42.1% of tokenized words to a vocab id as of the last build (up from 39.5% under the old per-token matcher), since compounds/conjugated forms now merge into one `GrammarExampleWord` (e.g. `通っている` → `通う`, `早かろうが` → `早い`) instead of splitting into unmatched fragments. Needs `build:data` to have run first (so `compiled/index/search.json` exists); not chained into `build:data` itself, since it's a small, independently-runnable pass, not another walk over the full sentence corpus.
- **`dataset/scripts/grammar-pattern-matcher.ts`**: for each example, matches `formation`'s literal Japanese against `words[]` (surface, `baseForm`, and reading, trying multiple formation-alternative variants and multi-token spans) to precompute `patternWordIndices` - which words are the point's literal grammar-pattern markers, as opposed to vocabulary filling its slots. Runs at build time specifically so `gokan-srs` never has to re-derive this at runtime; 99.9% of points have it located in at least one example as of the last build (one documented exception - see `gokan-dataset`'s pattern-location issue for the full methodology and the exception's rationale). Because `SentenceTokenizer`'s merging (above) can absorb a formation's literal marker into the middle of a merged conjugated word, `build-grammar.ts` runs `locatePattern` against a separate, fine-grained one-word-per-kuromoji-token array (never exposed in the compiled output) and maps the result back onto the merged `words[]` actually shipped - this is what keeps the 99.9%/98.1% pattern-location rate exactly at its pre-merge baseline despite the word-matching upgrade.
- **Output**: `compiled/grammar/points/{id}.json` (one `GrammarPoint` per file, mirroring `vocab/{id}.json`) and `compiled/grammar/index/jlpt.json` (level → ordered id list, mirroring `index/jlpt.json`'s shape). Synced into `public/data/compiled/grammar/` exactly like vocab/kanji/sentences - gitignored in `gokan-srs`, regenerated on demand.
- **Corpus-mined sentences (`compiled/grammar/mined/{id}.json`, issue #73)**: per eligible construction point, a `GrammarExample[]` of real vocab-corpus sentences that USE the point's construction, so the grammar quiz can pick the sentence that best exercises the vocabulary a learner is currently trying to produce (rather than only the 3-5 curated examples). Built by the dataset's `build-grammar-sentences.ts` (`build:grammar-sentences`, chained from `build:grammar`; needs `build:data` + `build:grammar` first), composing two precision gates: **`src/utils/formationMiner.ts`** (morphology-aware - compiles `formation` into an ordered `Element[]` rule of surface literals AND morphological constraints over kuromoji's POS / conjugation-form / 自立-vs-非自立 / base-form tags, so てある no longer matches である/がある: literal てある went 9,888→146 clean) decides IF an arbitrary sentence uses the construction, then the shared `buildExampleWords`/`locatePattern` places the blank exactly as for curated examples (dropping any sentence whose pattern can't be located). The miner compiles **every variant** of a formation (it used to keep only the longest literal, so `Verb-casual + から / … / Noun + だから` mined only だから) and enforces its **slots**: content before the marker (a sentence-initial しかし, whose A clause is not in the sentence, is rejected; so is the adverb 後で for `Verb-た + あとで`), typed content between literal groups (`Noun1 を Noun2 として` rejects 誘惑しようとして), and a following clause where the formation or a title ending in `～` requires one (sentence-final …んだから。 is rejected). A per-point marker lexicon built from the curated examples rejects homographs and re-segmented markers (the verb やら, 甘いものの量); connectives (`slot: sentence-initial`) must follow punctuation; answer leaks and texts over 60 chars or two sentences are dropped. High-precision points only (`isHighPrecision`; function-word over-matchers stay deferred): 16,023 examples across 522 points, pool capped at 60 (reservoir-sampled, each point from its own random stream seeded on its id, so changing one point never reshuffles another point's pool and the sentences learners had been seeing). Points with no eligible rule / deferred / zero verified matches simply have no file. See the dataset's `docs/SCHEMA.md`. **App consumption** (issue #73's app half, augment model: curated examples front the intro/first review, the mined pool powers productivity-driven reviews, curated fallback when a point has no pool) is wired into `computeBlankPlan` - see State Management -> Grammar Activity State's mined-selection note below.
- **Curriculum (`compiled/grammar/index/teaching-order.json`)**: the order points are **introduced** in, replacing `index/jlpt.json`'s alphabetical order for that purpose. Two docs in the dataset submodule cover this: `docs/GRAMMAR_TEACHING_MODEL.md` (hand-written, plain English: what a point, chapter, family, lesson and case each are and how they relate - read it before touching any of this) and `docs/CURRICULUM.md` (generated on every `build:grammar`, the full inventory of every chapter, its points, and the lessons it unlocks). Built by the dataset's `build-curriculum.ts` from an authored N5/N4 spine (`data/curriculum/chapters.json`, 40 hand-sequenced chapters) plus generated N3-N1 chapters. The app reads only the flat `order` array (`GrammarSRSService.getNextCandidates`/`countLearnableGrammar`) and `loadChapterFor`; the chapter arrangement itself is not surfaced in the UI yet. Two rules shape the N3-N1 half, both from the dataset's grammar-curriculum issue:
  - **Themes** (`data/curriculum/themes.json`, 42 authored chapters) replaced an alphabetical dump that put 315 points (42% of the dataset) into 18 buckets of 20 named "Further N2 patterns (3 of 5)". The fallback bucketing still exists so a point added upstream is visibly unthemed rather than silently dropped, and the build names every unthemed point; it is empty today. A theme keeps every point it lists, even one with a family: a per-level family chapter only collects members no theme lists. Once nearly every point had a family, letting family chapters claim themed points re-cut the themes into dozens of one- and two-point chapters. 148 chapters, median 4 points, max 12 (was 117, max 20).
  - **Absorb vs level-gate** for a family spread across levels: a *pure register ladder* (no `constraint` member) of at most 6 members becomes one chapter at its easiest member's level, so the ladder is taught whole; anything larger or mixed stays level-gated, with the level in the chapter title (`"N2: Concession (Even Though / Although / Despite)"`) so three chapters do not share one name. The size test is over the whole family, not just its register members - splitting a mixed family into an absorbed half and a level-gated half fragments it worse than either rule alone. 5 absorbed, 65 level-gated.
- **History**: the initial issue #17 implementation vendored this data directly into `gokan-srs` instead, because the autonomous agent's per-repo GitHub App installation token only had push access to `gokan-srs`, not the separate `gokan-dataset` repo - it had no way to open a PR there itself. Fixed by moving the raw data, build script, and compiled output into `gokan-dataset` (PR opened and merged by a maintainer working across both repos) and repointing `gokan-srs` at the updated submodule commit, consuming the output the same way as every other dataset.
